How answers are graded:
How answers are graded

- Each attempt is compared with your answer and marked right or wrong. The grade is on the whole attempt; the final answer shown is picked out automatically as a guide.
- Numbers match when they are the same value written differently: 0.675, 67.5% and 67.5 percentage points all match, and so do different scales (thousand, million, billion), thousands separators, currency symbols and numbers written as words.
- If the question asks for a particular format (a percentage, a number of decimal places, a unit, a rounding or an order), the attempt must meet it. If it asks for none, any equivalent form is accepted.
- For an ordered list, a title, honorific or article added to an entry is accepted if the order is right either with it or without it.
- Only the value the attempt finally commits to counts. Values reached while working, or considered and set aside, do not. An attempt that offers several values without choosing one is wrong, even if one of them is right.

Full answer
-----------

The measures Deloitte scores High cost / Low benefit are:

- Prohibit non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs (4.8b)
- Split UK firms into audit-only/non-audit practices (4.8c)
- Joint audits (4.18a)
- Break-up of the Big Four into smaller firms (4.27)
- Reform mandatory tendering and rotation (4.34)
- Insurance-based system (4.47)
- “NAO” style national auditor (4.50)

Of these, Grant Thornton says the following could leave the audit market less concentrated:

- Joint audits – GT says they “generally result in a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services” and can support an independent appointment body to broaden participation.
- Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation – GT argues tendering is “too infrequent to create a competitive market” and that more frequent rotation/mandatory switching after two terms would improve competition and reduce concentration.

Grant Thornton warns that the following could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs – GT warns that an overly broad ban must be “carefully defined” or it could result in “firms only being able to offer audits which were less effective” (i.e., lower quality).
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices – GT states that “audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights that are available to support the audit function on an ‘as needed’ basis in a multi-disciplinary firm” and that splitting them out risks losing specialist expertise and could cause some mid-tier firms to leave the market, weakening quality and resilience.

(Grant Thornton does not address the horizontal break-up of the Big Four, the insurance model, or the NAO-style auditor in terms of concentration or quality destabilisation in the visible response text; it rejects the latter two as unworkable/unrealistic.)

Let's locate specific paragraphs.

Doc 4 Executive Summary bullet points:
- Grant Thornton agrees competition not working well.
- Remedies adopted at end of CC investigation may have increased tendering but limited switching between Big 4, not non-Big 4 winning.
- Lack of effective competition central factor in GT decision to withdraw from future tendering for FTSE 350 audits.
- These problems specific to UK large company audit market, must be focus. Mid-sized and smaller companies operate in different market context; CMA should not impose measures disturbing well-functioning market or barriers to expansion.
- Reform must focus on strengthening independence of auditors of UK PIEs and removing biases in favour of Big 4.
- GT has technical capability, scale and capacity to undertake high quality audits for many FTSE 350 companies today including international operations. Nevertheless recognise largest and most complex companies, which may have systemic importance on international scale, will require auditor that can match that size and complexity, or two or more auditors acting under shared mandate.
- GT does not consider separating audit and non-audit practices of Big 4 effective remedy. Audit quality enhanced by broader resources and insights available in multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit unlikely to solve all concerns: rather create group of Big 4 audit-only firms, same market concentration and same buy-side biases. Some mid-tier firms may leave market, rather than split business to compete for PIE audit mandates. Although splitting out audit from non-audit business would partially address conflicts and independence, support simpler solutions.
- While GT benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory services, see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients. Regulatory changes tightened controls but auditors continue to provide non-audit services to PIE audit clients, often around 40% of audit fee (up to 70%). Important perception that sales create conflicts: clear ban may serve public interest by providing reassurance and promote trust. However certain services currently defined as non-audit but compatible with and complementary to high-quality audit (as outlined in response to q18). Any ban needs careful definition to ensure positive services allowed. Unfortunate if action resulted in firms only being able to offer audits less effective.
- GT does not consider introduction of market share cap in relation to percentage of FTSE 350 audits carried out by Big 4 is, by itself, useful tool for dealing with concerns. In particular not clear how this would operate without allowing Big 4 to cherry pick most attractive and profitable audits. GT does consider there may be some benefit in setting aspirational market share target for independent auditor appointment body to aim for.
- GT does consider advantages to joint or shared audits. Mechanism used in countries like France, where better market stability, fewer high-profile failures and greater public trust. Offers ability to draw on wider range of technical knowledge and experience, promotes better quality outcomes, ensures higher level of rigour and independence and generally results in more dynamic market in terms of number of firms able to provide effective audit services. Joint audits could be set up in number of ways – support shared audit model. Acknowledge joint audit criticised and resisted in past by buy-side, but look forward to discussing again. In particular consider introduction and use of joint audit is something that could be supported by independent auditor appointment body with public interest duty.
- GT previously called for independent auditor appointment body. Note use embraced in UK public sector. Equivalent mechanism for appointment of auditors to certain FTSE 250 companies could have positive effect in terms of promoting independence and addressing potential bias in procurement of audit by large companies in UK. Although significant change to current system, this would send clear signal UK serious about market reform and ensuring firm (or firms) appointed is best placed to deliver audit effectively, balancing interests of stakeholders and placing public interest at heart. Such arrangement could also deal with non-audit service procurement in fully independent manner addressing current concerns over conflicts (prohibition on selling non-audit services to PIE audit clients may also address). Do think private audit firms will provide best quality audit in well-functioning market: GT does not support nationalised provision of audit services.
- Effective reforms increasing competition and auditor independence in relation to PIEs strongly supported and would encourage more non-Big 4 firms to tender for FTSE 350 audits. Successful outcome to CMA study would be one where audit quality overall in FTSE 350 improves consistently; 10-20% of FTSE 350 audited by non-Big 4 within 3-4 years; wider stakeholder engagement; auditor independence strengthened; public trust restored.

"27. Should companies be required to tender their audits and rotate their auditors with greater frequency than they currently are required to do? What would be the costs and benefits of this?
27.1 Yes. As noted above, we consider that tendering is too infrequent to create a competitive market for audits to large companies in the UK market context, while overlong appointments undermine auditor scepticism and independence. Companies should be required to tender at least every five or seven years (as originally suggested by the CC), with mandatory switching after two consecutive terms.
27.2 The enhanced objectivity and independence of auditors, as created by such a change, will far outweigh the concern as to time needed to fully understand a client's business.
27.3 The consultation is mindful of increased costs presented by more frequent tender processes. We are of the view that these costs will be outweighed by the benefits:
• lengthier contracts may, over time, result in poor value for a client. Costs may be saved by more frequent reviews/appointments;
• the benefit of increased independence and higher quality audit, although more difficult to quantify, is likely to outweigh the cost of audit procurement; and
• we consider that reduced tender costs would be one of the advantages of independent appointment.
Grant Thornton looks forward..."

Full answer
-----------

The measures Deloitte scores as High cost / Low benefit are:

- Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices
- Joint audits
- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
- Insurance-based system
- “NAO”-style national auditor

Of these, Grant Thornton says the following could leave the audit market less concentrated:

- Prohibiting audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (GT notes Big Four might exit advisory work, letting non-Big Four grow, “potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition”).

Grant Thornton warns that the following could reduce or destabilise audit quality (alphabetically):

- “NAO”-style national auditor (GT warns of “likely destabilisation of audit quality, particularly in very large companies” and loss of staff/partners).
- Splitting UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices (GT states “audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights... in a multi-disciplinary firm” and that splitting out audit would not solve concerns; it also notes some mid-tier firms may leave the market).

The other high-cost/low-benefit measures (joint audits, break-up of the Big Four, reforming tendering/rotation, and an insurance-based system) are not cited by Grant Thornton in either of these specific ways.

Deloitte rates High cost / Low benefit for:

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
- Insurance-based system
- Joint audits
- “NAO” style national auditor
- Prohibit audit firms providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
- Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices

Of these, Grant Thornton says joint audits could make the market less concentrated (it says they can make the market “more dynamic in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services”).

Grant Thornton warns that the following could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs – it warns that an overly broad ban could leave firms “only being able to offer audits which were less effective”
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices – it says audit quality is “enhanced by the broader resources and insights... in a multi-disciplinary firm” and that splitting them out risks harming quality (and notes some mid-tier firms might leave the market)

So, alphabetically:

Could leave the market less concentrated:

- Joint audits

Could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices

Doc 4 sections:
Introduction: agree competition not working well, remedies adopted not impactful, Grant Thornton withdrew from FTSE350 tendering.

Executive Summary bullet points:
- Grant Thornton agrees competition not working well.
- Lack of effective competition central factor in decision to withdraw from tendering FTSE350.
- Problems specific to UK large company audit market, must be focus.
- Grant Thornton shares view unlikely one complete solution.
- Mid-sized and smaller companies operate in different market; CMA should not impose measures disturbing well-functioning market or barriers to expansion.
- Reform must focus on strengthening independence and removing biases in favour of Big 4.
- Grant Thornton has technical capability, scale, capacity to undertake high quality audits for many FTSE350 today including international ops. Recognise largest and most complex may require auditor matching size/complexity or two/more auditors under shared mandate.
- Grant Thornton does not consider separating audit and non-audit practices of Big 4 effective remedy. Audit quality enhanced by broader resources in multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit unlikely to solve concerns; rather create group of Big 4 audit-only firms, same concentration and same buy-side biases. Some mid-tier firms may leave market rather than split. Although splitting partially addresses conflicts and independence, support simpler solutions.
- While Grant Thornton benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory, see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients. Regulatory changes tightened controls but auditors continue to provide non-audit services to PIE audit clients, often around 40% of audit fee (up to 70%). Importantly perception that sales create conflicts: clear ban may serve public interest by providing reassurance and promote trust. However certain services currently defined as non-audit are compatible with and complementary to high-quality audit (as outlined in response to q18). Any ban needs careful definition to ensure positive services allowed to continue. Unfortunate if CMA action resulted in firms only being able to offer audits less effective.
- Grant Thornton does not consider introduction of market share cap by itself useful tool for dealing with concerns. Not clear how made to operate without allowing Big 4 to cherry pick most attractive/profitable audits. Does consider there may be some benefit in setting aspirational market share target for independent auditor appointment body (see below) to aim for.
- Grant Thornton does consider advantages to joint or shared audits. Mechanism used in countries like France, where better market stability, fewer high-profile failures, greater public trust. Offers ability to draw wider range of technical knowledge and experience, promotes better quality outcomes, ensures higher level of rigour and independence, generally results in more dynamic market in terms of number of firms able to provide effective audit services. Joint audits could be set up in number of ways – support shared audit model. Acknowledge joint audit criticised and resisted in past by buy-side, but look forward to discussing with CMA. In particular, introduction and use of joint audit something that could be supported by independent auditor appointment body with public interest duty.
- Grant Thornton previously called for independent auditor appointment body. Note use embraced in UK public sector. Equivalent mechanism for appointment of auditors to certain FTSE250 companies could have positive effect in terms of promoting independence and addressing potential bias. Although significant change, would send clear signal UK serious about market reform and ensuring firm appointed is best placed to deliver audit balancing interests of stakeholders and placing public interest at heart. Such arrangement could also deal with non-audit service procurement in fully independent manner addressing current concerns over conflicts (prohibition on selling non-audit services to PIE audit clients may also address this). Do not support nationalised provision of audit services.
- Effective reforms increasing competition and auditor independence in relation to PIEs strongly supported and would encourage more non-Big 4 firms to tender for FTSE350 audits. Successful outcome to CMA study would be one where: audit quality overall in FTSE350 improves consistently; 10-20% FTSE350 audited by non-Big 4 within 3-4 years; wider stakeholder engagement with audit; auditor independence strengthened; public trust restored.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the provided documents, the following analysis identifies the relevant measures from the CMA's potential measures that Deloitte rates as "High" for costs and "Low" for benefits (per Deloitte's summary table in Document 2, Section 14.4), and then determines Grant Thornton's position on each per Document 4.

Deloitte's High-Cost/Low-Benefit Measures (from Document 2, Section 14.4 table):

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE (4.8(b))
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))
- Joint audits (4.18(a))
- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27)
- Insurance-based system (4.47)
- "NAO" style national auditor (4.50)

Grant Thornton's Assessment (Document 4):

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27): In Annex 2, Grant Thornton lists "Immediate impact on market concentration" as a benefit, indicating this measure would reduce concentration.
- Joint audits (4.18(a)): Explicitly states joint audits "may also create opportunities for non-Big 4 firms by challenging supply-side barriers to entry" and references the French market as "the least concentrated in Europe" due to this model.

- Joint audits (4.18(a)): Annex 2 warns "Non-Big 4 may not have capacity/skills to deliver joint audits at larger organisations. Could lead to disputes over liability if audits are challenged."
- "NAO" style national auditor (4.50): Annex 2 explicitly states "Loss of partners and staff in the market who would leave for more lucrative employment and likely destabilisation of audit quality, particularly in very large companies."
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c)): States "With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded (and the principal-agent concern more entrenched)."

"Grant Thornton does not consider that separating the audit and non-audit practices of the Big 4 would be an effective remedy. Audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights that are available to support the audit function on an "as needed" basis in a multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit is unlikely to solve all the concerns: rather it would create a group of Big 4 audit-only firms, with the same level of market concentration and the same buy-side biases in play. Some mid-tier firms may leave the market, rather than split their business to compete for PIE audit mandates. Although splitting out audit from non-audit business would partially address conflicts of interest and independence, we support simpler solutions."

This seems to be referring to measure 4.8(c) "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices" rather than 4.8(b).

Let me look for 4.8(b) specifically: "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs"

From Document 4:
"While Grant Thornton benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory services to clients, we see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients. Regulatory changes which have tightened controls over the selling of both audit and non-audit services to PIEs have reduced the practice, but auditors continue to provide non-audit services to their PIE audit clients, often to the value of around 40 per cent of the audit fee (and up to a limit of 70%). Importantly, there is a perception that sales of non-audit services create conflicts of interest: a clear ban may serve the public interest by providing wider stakeholders with reassurance and promote trust in the industry. However, there are certain services which are currently defined as 'non-audit', but are compatible with and, indeed, complementary to, the provision of high-quality audit (as outlined in our response to question 18, below). Any 'ban' on firms receiving non-audit fees from such clients would need to be carefully defined to ensure that these positive services are allowed to continue. It would be unfortunate if any action by the CMA resulted in firms only being able to offer audits which were less effective."

This is about prohibiting non-audit services to PIE audit clients, which seems to be measure 4.8(a) "Non-audit services ban to audit clients" not 4.8(b) "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE".

From Deloitte's table:
- Non-audit services ban to audit clients (4.8(a)): Medium costs, High benefits
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE (4.8(b)): High costs, Low benefits
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c)): High costs, Low benefits

So 4.8(b) is prohibiting non-audit services to ANY large company or PIE, not just audit clients.

"18.1 We recommend restricting the provision of services to PIE audit clients in the UK, other than those that are strictly necessary to complete an audit or that closely complement the audit. Services currently defined as 'non-audit', but which it is in the interests of stakeholders for auditors to provide, are:
- reporting required by law or regulation to be provided by the auditor;
- reviews of interim financial information;
- reporting on regulatory returns;
- reporting to a regulator on client assets:

"18.3 As noted in response to question 16 above, we consider that full separation of audit and non-audit firms would not create positive outcomes, and, as a measure designed to reduce conflicts of interest in relation to audits to the largest companies, may have negative outcomes for mid-sized and smaller companies."

"18.4 We suggest that few mid-tier firms would be willing to adopt the costlier 'audit-only' operating model for the purposes of serving some PIE clients. A likely outcome would be an increase in UK market concentration as mid-tier firms withdraw from the market for audit to large companies and PIEs."

"18.5 Although we acknowledge concerns that some large companies face a limited choice of auditor due to other auditors being 'conflicted out', we consider that, for most of FTSE 350 companies and PIEs, this problem can be remedied by improving competitive conditions so that mid-tier firms provide credible audit alternatives. If the Big 4 were forced to split, their audit and advisory businesses would be freed of their respective conflicts of interest. This would simply reinforce their collective dominance of the relevant markets. As we explain at paragraph 16.4 above, creating 'audit-only' firms may also exacerbate the existing 'principal-agent' problem."

"18.6 We think that concerns around conflicts can be better resolved through restrictions on the provision of audit and non-audit services to a single client (save for non-audit services that are necessary to perform the audit). We also believe that the creation of an independent audit appointments body, described below, could be used to authorise non-audit services, which would have the further benefit of addressing the inherent conflict within current arrangements."

This seems to be discussing measure 4.8(c) "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices" rather than 4.8(b).

Next measure: "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices" (4.8(c))

From Document 4, section 16: "One way to create audit-only firms would be through separate ownership of the audit and non-audit services practices of the UK audit firms. Could this be effective, and what would be the relative scale of benefits and costs?"

"16.1 As noted above, Grant Thornton does not support the separation of ownership of audit and non-audit service practices of UK audit firms. We think that there are a number of positive features of having firms with both capacities and are sceptical as to whether there would be any material benefit in splitting out the firms, whilst we suggest it would be difficult to achieve on a UK-only basis."

"16.2 Benefits of firms offering both audit and non-audit services include:
- efficiencies being generated across full-service firms, whereby access is gained to non-audit sector and field specialists, who may be required infrequently for audit mandates, but are able to contribute valuable skills and knowledge where relevant; and
- the ability for firms to offer a wider range of professional opportunities to attract greater talent, as well as enhancing the breadth of the training provided to individuals."

"16.3 On the other hand, we do not think that the creation of audit-only firms would solve the systemic issues in the market. It is unlikely that separating UK ownership would improve competitive conditions in the market; the concerns we have outlined above, which prompted our withdrawal from the audit market for FTSE 350 companies, would remain, but in respect of an 'audit-only' Big 4."

"16.4 Some issues may be exacerbated by an 'audit-only' market. With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded (and the principal-agent concern more entrenched). There may also be unintended consequences for businesses outside the FTSE 350. Mid-market and smaller companies benefit from looking to auditors for a more holistic service that encompasses wider services and advice, while separate procurement of audit and non-audit services may also be commercially unrealistic and onerous for these companies."

"16.5 The full separation of UK audit and non-audit companies would be costly and time-consuming to implement. It would also be excessive in light of the more moderate (but arguably equally effective) remedies available to address 'conflicts' issues, such as banning advisory sales to PIE audit clients."

"16.6 Ring-fencing of the audit business as a separate economic entity within firms that audit PIEs has been proposed as a solution to conflicts of interest. We see this as challenging to implement and monitor, as well as being a weak response to the concern around conflicts; one that is also unlikely to mitigate the perception of conflict. A simple ban on selling properly and carefully defined non-audit services to PIE audit clients would be more effective and transparent. In addition, whilst Big 4 firms may receive sufficient PIE audit business to justify a ring-fenced audit practice, we consider that such arrangements could have the consequence of excluding non-Big 4 firms from the PIE audit market. This is because the audit portfolios of Non-Big 4 firms more heavily reflect non-PIE companies who benefit from full-service teams and whose ability to prosper would be diminished if they could only access the ring-fenced audit services of a firm. The likely exit of non-Big 4 firms as a result of ring-fencing requirements would protect concentration and undermine competition in the PIE audit market."

This is very relevant. Grant Thornton says that splitting into audit-only firms (4.8(c)) "would protect concentration and undermine competition in the PIE audit market" - meaning it would make the market MORE concentrated, not less. But the question asks which measures Grant Thornton says "could leave the audit market less concentrated". So this measure would do the opposite.

Wait, let me read carefully: "The likely exit of non-Big 4 firms as a result of ring-fencing requirements would protect concentration and undermine competition in the PIE audit market."

"Protect concentration" means maintain or increase concentration. So this measure would INCREASE concentration, not decrease it.

But the question asks for measures that "could leave the audit market less concentrated". So this measure (4.8(c)) is not one of them.

Let me check the next measure: Joint audits (4.18(a))

From Document 4, section 15.2 onwards:

"15.2 Further to the analysis provided at Annex 2, Grant Thornton considers that joint audits for UK PIEs would help to address certain CMA and public interest concerns, particularly if implemented in conjunction with other remedies such as independent appointments."

"15.3 We acknowledge that joint audits have been resisted by industry in the past. Common criticisms have included that previous efforts to implement joint audits in the UK have been ineffective and that joint audit can lead to a duplication of audit work (and cost)."

"15.4 Nonetheless, joint audit has a number of positive aspects. In particular, joint audit can create better quality audits by combining the complementary technical expertise, sector insight, experience and geographical reach of two or more audit firms. Joint audit could also create options for the largest companies, who, for reasons discussed above, may otherwise have very restricted choice of auditor. Joint audit may also create opportunities for non-Big 4 firms by challenging supply-side barriers to entry and giving firms more experience of large company and PIE audit work, making them more competitive participants in future FTSE 350 audit procurement. In sum, these positive aspects of joint audit may also improve resilience in the market."

"15.5 We are of the view that costs related to the use of joint audit can be outweighed by these benefits and mitigated or eliminated through design. Concerns around duplication of work and cost stem, we think, from a view of joint audit consisting of two firms conducting an audit and jointly signing-off group accounts. A more effective model (and one that is closer to the model operated to good effect in France) might be termed 'shared audit'."

"15.6 A shared audit model enables work to be divided and shared. This can be organised in a sensible way depending upon the audit requirements, including by risk area, subsidiary and geography etc. However, there is scope for overlap where useful, for example in relation to particularly complex matters or operations for which an entity might seek a contribution from each joint auditor. During an audit tenure there can be periodic rotation of audit procedures, meaning that some elements can be approached with 'fresh eyes' and an audit is not reliant upon an auditor identifying their own past oversights."

"15.7 A shared audit model has been implemented successfully in the French market. There is no clear empirical evidence that this has led to any increase in costs to business. The French audit market is, however, the least concentrated in Europe, with Mazars and our French affiliate firm acting as credible competitors to the Big 4. We understand that public perception of and trust in audit is greater in the French market, whilst there are fewer instances of significant company failures. Overall, the system appears to serve the public interest well."

This suggests that joint audits (or shared audits) could REDUCE concentration (make the market less concentrated) by creating opportunities for non-Big 4 firms.

Also, in Annex 2 (which is referenced but not fully included in the text), it says for "Variations of joint and shared audits": "Works in other countries. Effective at promoting independence. Shared audits offer flexible alternative - easier for non-Big 4 to undertake and can avoid duplication of work/cost. Takes advantage of complimentary specialisms"

And for costs: "Joint could be high cost and complex. Non-Big 4 may not have capacity/skills to deliver joint audits at larger organisations. Could lead to disputes over liability if audits are challenged"

So Grant Thornton believes joint/shared audits could reduce concentration (make market less concentrated) but warns about potential costs and quality issues.

Full answer
-----------

Looking at Deloitte's summary table (Document 2, section 14.4), the measures rated High for costs and Low for benefits are:

1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b))
2. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))
3. Joint audits (4.18(a))
4. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27)

Measures Grant Thornton says could leave the audit market less concentrated (alphabetical):

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms — In Annex 2, Grant Thornton lists as a benefit: "Immediate impact on market concentration."
- Joint audits — In its response to Q23, Grant Thornton notes that joint audit "is common in France and is encouraged by the regulatory regime. This does appear to have created a more dynamic and less concentrated market (the top four firms have only c. 47% of the market)."
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs — In Annex 2, Grant Thornton lists as a benefit: "Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

Measures Grant Thornton warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality (alphabetical):

- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices — In Annex 2, Grant Thornton explicitly warns under costs: "Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality." In the main body (Q16), it also notes that an audit-only market could erode "independence and incentives to challenge audit clients" and that "Some issues may be exacerbated by an 'audit-only' market."

(Note: Grant Thornton does not warn that the Prohibit NAS measure, Joint audits, or Break-up of the Big Four would reduce or destabilise audit quality — in fact, it characterises joint audits as creating "better quality audits" and the prohibition measure as potentially encouraging "investment in audit quality.")

3. "Joint audits" (4.18(a)) - High costs, Low benefits
4. "Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms" (4.27) - High costs, Low benefits

- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c)): Costs - High, Benefits - Low
- Joint audits (4.18(a)): Costs - High, Benefits - Low
- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27): Costs - High, Benefits - Low

Grant Thornton explicitly says "Big 4 audit firms remain so no impact to market concentration" - meaning it won't reduce concentration.

Grant Thornton says shared audits "Works in other countries" and "Effective at promoting independence."

Wait, but in the main body of Grant Thornton's response (section 16), they discuss audit-only firms and say "the creation of audit-only firms would not solve the systemic issues in the market" and "It is unlikely that separating UK ownership would improve competitive conditions in the market."

Let me re-read the question carefully:
"Of the CMA's potential measures that Deloitte rates High for costs and Low for benefits, which, if any, does Grant Thornton say could leave the audit market less concentrated, and which does it warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality?"

So I need to identify:
1. Which measures (among those Deloitte rates High cost/Low benefit) does Grant Thornton say could leave the market LESS concentrated?
2. Which measures (among those Deloitte rates High cost/Low benefit) does Grant Thornton warn could REDUCE or DESTABILISE audit quality?

The four measures rated High cost/Low benefit by Deloitte are:
A) Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b))
B) Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))
C) Joint audits (4.18(a))

Now checking Grant Thornton on concentration:

So for "leaves the audit market less concentrated":

- B (Split firms): No, Grant Thornton says no impact on concentration
- C (Joint audits): Grant Thornton suggests it could increase opportunities for non-Big 4 firms, but doesn't explicitly say "less concentrated" in the same way. Let me look more carefully...
  Actually, in Annex 2 Measure 5, benefits include: "Works in other countries" and "Effective at promoting independence" and "Takes advantage of complimentary specialisms". They don't explicitly say "less concentrated" here.

Do they say it could reduce or destabilise audit quality? They say that without non-audit specialists, audit quality might be affected. We consider that this insurance remedy would be unworkable for this reason, though we also suspect that audit costs would increase significantly if auditors were to be expected to provide a level of assurance that insured a company against deterioration in its share value." - Break link between company management and auditors (4.40): Low costs, Low benefits, High implementation challenges (but costs are Low, not High)
- Insurance-based system (4.47): High costs, Low benefits, High implementation challenges

First, let's look for where Grant Thornton discusses market concentration effects.

In Document 4, under "Executive Summary" bullet point 5:
"Grant Thornton does not consider that the introduction of a market share cap in relation to the percentage of audits of FTSE 350 companies carried out by the Big 4 is, by itself, a useful tool for dealing with the concerns that have been identified. In particular, it is not clear to us how this would be made to operate without allowing the Big 4 to cherry pick the most attractive and profitable audits."

But market share cap isn't one of the measures Deloitte rated High cost/Low benefit.

Let me look for the specific measures from Deloitte's "do not support" list.

Looking at section 16 in Document 4: "One way to create audit-only firms would be through separate ownership of the audit and non-audit services practices of the UK audit firms. Could this be effective, and what would be the relative scale of benefits and costs?"

This corresponds to Deloitte's "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))"

Grant Thornton's response in section 16.1: "As noted above, Grant Thornton does not support the separation of ownership of audit and non-audit service practices of UK audit firms. We think that there are a number of positive features of having firms with both capacities and are sceptical as to whether there would be any material benefit in splitting out the firms, whilst we suggest it would be difficult to achieve on a UK-only basis."

Section 16.3: "On the other hand, we do not think that the creation of audit-only firms would solve the systemic issues in the market. It is unlikely that separating UK ownership would improve competitive conditions in the market; the concerns we have outlined above, which prompted our withdrawal from the audit market for FTSE 350 companies, would remain, but in respect of an 'audit-only' Big 4."

Section 16.4: "Some issues may be exacerbated by an 'audit-only' market. With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded (and the principal-agent concern more entrenched). There may also be unintended consequences for businesses outside the FTSE 350. Mid-market and smaller companies benefit from looking to auditors for a more holistic service that encompasses wider services and advice, while separate procurement of audit and non-audit services may also be commercially unrealistic and onerous for these companies."

Section 16.5: "The full separation of UK audit and non-audit companies would be costly and time-consuming to implement. It would also be excessive in light of the more moderate (but arguably equally effective) remedies available to address 'conflicts' issues, such as banning advisory sales to PIE audit clients."

Section 16.6: "Ring-fencing of the audit business as a separate economic entity within firms that audit PIEs has been proposed as a solution to conflicts of interest. We see this as challenging to implement and monitor, as well as being a weak response to the concern around conflicts; one that is also unlikely to mitigate the perception of conflict. A simple ban on selling properly and carefully defined non-audit services to PIE audit clients would be more effective and transparent. In addition, whilst Big 4 firms may receive sufficient PIE audit business to justify a ring-fenced audit practice, we consider that such arrangements could have the consequence of excluding non-Big 4 firms from the PIE audit market. This is because the audit portfolios of Non-Big 4 firms more heavily reflect non-PIE companies who benefit from full-service teams and whose ability to prosper would be diminished if they could only access the ring-fenced audit services of a firm. The likely exit of non-Big 4 firms as a result of ring-fencing requirements would protect concentration and undermine competition in the PIE audit market."

Ah, here's something important! In 16.6, Grant Thornton says: "The likely exit of non-Big 4 firms as a result of ring-fencing requirements would protect concentration and undermine competition in the PIE audit market."

Ring-fencing is related to splitting audit and non-audit practices (measure 4.8(c)), but it's not exactly the same as full separation. However, this suggests that Grant Thornton believes splitting/ring-fencing would INCREASE concentration (protect concentration), not decrease it.

Let me check the other measures.

Section 15.2-15.7 discusses joint audits:
"15.2 Further to the analysis provided at Annex 2, Grant Thornton considers that joint audits for UK PIEs would help to address certain CMA and public interest concerns, particularly if implemented in conjunction with other remedies such as independent appointments.

15.3 We acknowledge that joint audits have been resisted by industry in the past. Common criticisms have included that previous efforts to implement joint audits in the UK have been ineffective and that joint audit can lead to a duplication of audit work (and cost).

15.4 Nonetheless, joint audit has a number of positive aspects. In particular, joint audit can create better quality audits by combining the complementary technical expertise, sector insight, experience and geographical reach of two or more audit firms. Joint audit could also create options for the largest companies, who, for reasons discussed above, may otherwise have very restricted choice of auditor. Joint audit may also create opportunities for non-Big 4 firms by challenging supply-side barriers to entry and giving firms more experience of large company and PIE audit work, making them more competitive participants in future FTSE 350 audit procurement. In sum, these positive aspects of joint audit may also improve resilience in the market.

15.5 We are of the view that costs related to the use of joint audit can be outweighed by these benefits and mitigated or eliminated through design. Concerns around duplication of work and cost stem, we think, from a view of joint audit consisting of two firms conducting an audit and jointly signing-off group accounts. A more effective model (and one that is closer to the model operated to good effect in France) might be termed 'shared audit'.

15.6 A shared audit model enables work to be divided and shared. This can be organised in a sensible way depending upon the audit requirements, including by risk area, subsidiary and geography etc. However, there is scope for overlap where useful, for example in relation to particularly complex matters or operations for which an entity might seek a contribution from each joint auditor. During an audit tenure there can be periodic rotation of audit procedures, meaning that some elements can be approached with 'fresh eyes' and an audit is not reliant upon an auditor identifying their own past oversights.

15.7 A shared audit model has been implemented successfully in the French market. There is no clear empirical evidence that this has led to any increase in costs to business. The French audit market is, however, the least concentrated in Europe, with Mazars and our French affiliate firm acting as credible competitors to the Big 4. We understand that public perception of and trust in audit is greater in the French market, whilst there are fewer instances of significant company failures. Overall, the system appears to serve the public interest well."

This is very relevant! Grant Thornton says joint audits (and shared audits) have been successful in France, where "the French audit market is, however, the least concentrated in Europe". So joint audits could leave the audit market less concentrated.

Also, they say joint audits "can create better quality audits" and "may also improve resilience in the market." They don't warn that joint audits could reduce or destabilise audit quality - in fact, they argue the opposite.

Let me check the break-up of Big 4 measure.

Section 14.5.13 in Deloitte's document (but that's Deloitte, not Grant Thornton). Let me search Grant Thornton's document for "break up" or "break-up".

In Document 4, section 16.3: "It is unlikely that separating UK ownership would improve competitive conditions in the market; the concerns we have outlined above, which prompted our withdrawal from the audit market for FTSE 350 companies, would remain, but in respect of an 'audit-only' Big 4."

But this is about audit-only firms, not breaking up the Big 4 into smaller firms.

Let me search for "break up the Big Four" or similar.

In Annex 2 of Document 4 (which is referenced but not fully included in the text provided), there's a table with "Measure Costs Benefits" and row 9 is "Break up the Big 4 into smaller firms".

But the detailed text for this isn't fully provided in the excerpt. Let me check if there's more.

In the main text of Document 4, I don't see a detailed discussion of breaking up the Big 4 into smaller firms, only the audit-only firm separation.

Let me check section 15.1: "For responses to questions 14 and 15, please see Annex 2, attached. This considers the costs and benefits of the measures in Section 4 of the ITC and suggests further measures that the CMA should consider to address the issues highlighted in Section 3 of the ITC."

And Annex 2 is described as "ANNEX 2 – COSTS AND BENEFITS OF POTENTIAL MEASURES" but only a partial table is shown in the document.

But this is just a table header/benefit column, not Grant Thornton's detailed view.

Let me look for more in Grant Thornton's text.

In the Executive Summary: "Grant Thornton does not consider that separating the audit and non-audit practices of the Big 4 would be an effective remedy. Audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights that are available to support the audit function on an "as needed" basis in a multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit is unlikely to solve all the concerns: rather it would create a group of Big 4 audit-only firms, with the same level of market concentration and the same buy-side biases in play. Some mid-tier firms may leave the market, rather than split their business to compete for PIE audit mandates."

This is about splitting audit and non-audit (measure 4.8(c)), not breaking up Big 4 into smaller firms (measure 4.27).

Let me search for "4.27" in Grant Thornton's document.

I don't see explicit reference to "4.27" but the measures are referenced by their CMA section numbers.

In the CMA's Invitation to Comment, section 4.27 is "Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms" as seen in Deloitte's table.

In Grant Thornton's Annex 2 table, measure 9 is "Break up the Big 4 into smaller firms" with benefit "Immediate impact on market concentration".

Given that Grant Thornton included this in their analysis and listed "Immediate impact on market concentration" as a benefit, it suggests they believe this measure would reduce concentration.

Now for the second part: which measure does Grant Thornton warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality?

From the joint audit discussion, Grant Thornton seems positive about joint audits for quality.

From the audit-only firm discussion (16.4): "Some issues may be exacerbated by an 'audit-only' market. With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded (and the principal-agent concern more entrenched)."

This suggests audit-only firms (measure 4.8(c)) could reduce audit quality by eroding independence and incentives to challenge.

Also in 16.6: "Ring-fencing of the audit business as a separate economic entity within firms that audit PIEs has been proposed as a solution to conflicts of interest. We see this as challenging to implement and monitor, as well as being a weak response to the concern around conflicts; one that is also unlikely to mitigate the perception of conflict."

But ring-fencing is slightly different from full separation.

Let me check the other measures.

For "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs" (4.8(b)):

In Executive Summary: "While Grant Thornton benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory services to clients, we see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients. Regulatory changes which have tightened controls over the selling of both audit and non-audit services to PIEs have reduced the practice, but auditors continue to provide non-audit services to their PIE audit clients, often to the value of around 40 per cent of the audit fee (and up to a limit of 70%). Importantly, there is a perception that sales of non-audit services create conflicts of interest: a clear ban may serve the public interest by providing wider stakeholders with reassurance and promote trust in the industry. However, there are certain services which are currently defined as 'non-audit', but are compatible with and, indeed, complementary to, the provision of high-quality audit (as outlined in our response to question 18, below). Any 'ban' on firms receiving non-audit fees from such clients would need to be carefully defined to ensure that these positive services are allowed to continue. It would be unfortunate if any action by the CMA resulted in firms only being able to offer audits which were less effective."

So they support a ban but with caveats, and warn that a poorly defined ban "resulted in firms only being able to offer audits which were less effective" - so could reduce audit quality if not carefully defined.

But they don't outright say it would reduce quality; they say it could if not done right.

For insurance-based system and NAO-style national auditor, let me check.

In section 26.5: "The consultation presents two further options: a new insurance model related to share price; and the creation of an independent 'NAO-style' national auditor which could replace the auditors of some or all of the large companies and PIEs (i.e. nationalise large company audit). Grant Thornton does not support either measure:
- there are many factors that inform share price and it would be exceptionally difficult to accurately link stock market performance with the quality of an audit, even when supported by judgements of the regulator. We consider that this insurance remedy would be unworkable for this reason, though we also suspect that audit costs would increase significantly if auditors were to be expected to provide a level of assurance that insured a company against deterioration in its share value; and
- we essentially agree with the each of the concerns raised against an NAO-style auditor, as set out at paragraph 4.51 of the ITC. We do not think that this is a realistic solution, nor one that stakeholders would support."

For insurance-based system, they say it would be "unworkable" and "audit costs would increase significantly" but don't explicitly say it would reduce audit quality.

For NAO-style, they reference CMA's concerns but don't detail them in their response.

Now, let's go back to the two specific questions:

- Joint audits (4.18(a)): Grant Thornton says "The French audit market is, however, the least concentrated in Europe" with joint audits, so this could leave market less concentrated.
- Break up Big 4 into smaller firms (4.27): Grant Thornton's Annex 2 lists "Immediate impact on market concentration" as a benefit, implying reduced concentration.

2. Which measure does Grant Thornton warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality?

- Audit-only firms / split audit and non-audit (4.8(c)): Grant Thornton says "With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded (and the principal-agent concern more entrenched)." This suggests reduced audit quality.
- Prohibit non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs (4.8(b)): Grant Thornton warns "It would be unfortunate if any action by the CMA resulted in firms only being able to offer audits which were less effective." So could reduce quality if not carefully done.
- But the strongest warning is for audit-only firms.

Full answer
-----------

Deloitte's High-Cost / Low-Benefit Measures

From Deloitte's summary table (14.4), the measures rated High costs and Low benefits are:

1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
2. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices
3. Joint audits
4. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
5. Insurance-based system
6. "NAO"-style national auditor

---

Grant Thornton says could leave the audit market less concentrated:

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE — GT's Annex 2 (measure 2) states as a benefit: "Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

---

Grant Thornton warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

- "NAO"-style national auditor — GT's Annex 2 (measure 15) lists as a cost: "Loss of partners and staff in the market who would leave for more lucrative employment and likely destabilisation of audit quality, particularly in very large companies."
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices — GT's Annex 2 (measure 3) lists as a cost: "Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality."

4. List each group alphabetically

Now, what does Grant Thornton say about these measures? Let me look at Grant Thornton's Annex 2 table and their main response.

From Grant Thornton's Annex 2:

1. **Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE** (measure 2 in Annex 2):
   - Costs: "Firms will leave the market if they lose lucrative consulting work at large organisations. Reduces the incentive for non-Big 4 to enter the large company audit market."
   - Benefits: "Audit is valued more by the audit firm – encourages investment in audit quality. Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

2. **Split the UK arms of major accounting firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices** (measure 3 in Annex 2):
   - Costs: "Complex for mid-market companies who value full service offering. Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality. All other costs of 1 above."
   - Benefits: "Big 4 audit firms remain so no impact to market concentration. Non-conflicted Big 4 could dominate market even more than under current arrangements."

3. **Joint audits** (measure 5 in Annex 2):
   - Costs: "Joint could be high cost and complex. Non-Big 4 may not have capacity/skills to deliver joint audits at larger organisations. Could lead to disputes over liability if audits are challenged."
   - Benefits: "Works in other countries. Effective at promoting independence. Shared audits offer flexible alternative – easier for non-Big 4 to undertake and can avoid duplication of work/cost. Takes advantage of complimentary specialisms."

4. **Break up the Big 4 into smaller firms** (measure 9 in Annex 2):
   - Costs: "Complicated to determine how the splits could be made – where would NAS sit etc? Difficult to achieve for global organisations – which organisation would be part of the group?"
   - Benefits: "Immediate impact on market concentration"

5. **Insurance-based system for audit** (measure 14 in Annex 2):
   - Costs: "High cost. Difficult to achieve with limited liability. Amount of audit work increases to potentially unworkable levels. Difficult to achieve when international arrangements are impacted."
   - Benefits: (not listed in Annex 2 table as far as I can see)

Measures Deloitte Rates High Cost / Low Benefit, Cross-Referenced with Grant Thornton

Deloitte's High-cost, Low-benefit measures (from the table at 14.4) are:

1. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27)
2. Insurance-based system (4.47)
3. Joint audits (4.18(a))
4. NAO-style national auditor (4.50)
5. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b))
6. Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation (4.34)
7. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))

---

Could leave the market less concentrated (per Grant Thornton, Annex 2)

| Measure | GT's language | |---|---| | Break up the Big 4 into smaller firms | Benefit listed: "Immediate impact on market concentration" | | Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs | Benefit listed: "Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition" |

---

Could reduce or destabilise audit quality (per Grant Thornton, Annex 2)

| Measure | GT's language | |---|---| | NAO-style national auditor | Cost listed: "Loss of partners and staff in the market who would leave for more lucrative employment and likely destabilisation of audit quality, particularly in very large companies" | | Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices | Cost listed: "Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality" |

The remaining three Deloitte High/Low measures (insurance-based system, joint audits, reform mandatory tendering) are not flagged by Grant Thornton in either category.

Full answer
-----------

Identifying Deloitte's High Cost / Low Benefit Measures

From Deloitte's summary table (¶14.4), the measures rated High costs and Low benefits are:

1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8b)
2. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8c)
3. Joint audits (4.18a)
4. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27)
5. Insurance-based system (4.47)
6. "NAO"-style national auditor (4.50)

Grant Thornton's Stance on These Measures

Measures GT says could leave the audit market less concentrated:

Grant Thornton's Annex 2 (item 2) states that prohibiting NAS to all large companies and PIEs could lead to "less audit market concentration and more competition" as Big 4 firms leave to concentrate on advisory work. On break-up (Annex 2, item 9), GT lists "Immediate impact on market concentration" as a benefit.

- Break-up of the Big Four into smaller audit firms
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs

---

Measures GT warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

Grant Thornton's Annex 2 (item 3) warns that splitting firms would cause "Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality." For the NAO-style auditor (Annex 2, item 15), GT warns of "likely destabilisation of audit quality, particularly in very large companies."

- NAO-style national auditor
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices

Reasoning
---------

Let me break down the question:

1. First, I need to find the measures that Deloitte rates "High" for costs AND "Low" for benefits (from Deloitte's summary table at 14.4).

From Deloitte's table (14.4):

Measures rated "High" costs AND "Low" benefits:
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b)): Costs=High, Benefits=Low
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c)): Costs=High, Benefits=Low
- Joint audits (4.18(a)): Costs=High, Benefits=Low
- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27): Costs=High, Benefits=Low
- Break link between company management and auditors (4.40): Costs=Low, Benefits=Low — wait, let me re-check. It says Low for costs, Low for benefits. So this doesn't qualify.

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b)): High, Low, High
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c)): High, Low, High
- Joint audits (4.18(a)): High, Low, Medium
- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27): High, Low, High
- Break link between company management and auditors (4.40): Low, Low, High
- Insurance-based system (4.47): High, Low, High

1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs

3. Joint audits
4. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
5. Insurance-based system
6. "NAO" style national auditor

Now, from Grant Thornton's response, I need to find which of these measures Grant Thornton says could leave the audit market less concentrated, and which it warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality.

Let me go through Grant Thornton's document:

**Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs:**
In Annex 2, item 2: "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE"
Costs: "Firms will leave the market if they lose lucrative consulting work at large organisations. Reduces the incentive for non-Big 4 to enter the large company audit market."
Benefits: "Audit is valued more by the audit firm – encourages investment in audit quality. Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

So GT says this could lead to "less audit market concentration and more competition." This is about leaving the market less concentrated.

**Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices:**
In Annex 2, item 3: Costs include "Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality." And "All other costs of 1 above."
Benefits include "Ringfencing could be a reasonable middle ground...but will lose some of the efficiencies of full-service firms" and "All other benefits of 1 above."

GT says splitting could reduce audit quality (loss of technical specialisms, restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality).

Also in the main text (16.1): "Grant Thornton does not support the separation of ownership of audit and non-audit service practices of UK audit firms." And (16.6): "Ring-fencing...could have the consequence of excluding non-Big 4 firms from the PIE audit market."

**Joint audits:**
In Annex 2, item 5: "Variations of joint and shared audits"
Costs: "Joint could be high cost and complex. Non-Big 4 may not have capacity/skills to deliver joint audits at larger organisations. Could lead to disputes over liability if audits are challenged."
Benefits: "Works in other countries. Effective at promoting independence. Shared audits offer flexible alternative..."

GT supports joint/shared audits. They don't really warn it reduces quality; they see benefits.

- joint audit
- market share caps

And it warns that the one which could reduce or destabilise audit quality is:

- operational separation (the structural split of audit from non-audit services)

Full answer

The measures Deloitte scores High cost / Low benefit are:

- Prohibiting audit firms from providing non-audit services to any large company or PIE (regardless of audit)
- Splitting UK firms into audit-only and non-audit practices
- Joint audits
- Breaking up the Big Four into smaller audit firms
- Reforming mandatory tendering and auditor rotation
- An insurance-based system
- An “NAO-style” national auditor

Of these, Grant Thornton mentions only joint audits, the prohibition on non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs, and splitting firms into audit-only/non-audit practices.

Could leave the market less concentrated

- Joint audits (Grant Thornton cites France’s shared-audit model as producing “the least concentrated” audit market in Europe)

Could reduce or destabilise audit quality (alphabetically)

- Joint audits (Grant Thornton opposes them “on cost and quality grounds”, warns issues can “fall between the cracks” and that there is “no evidence that joint audits increase quality”)
- Prohibiting audit firms from providing non-audit services to any large company or PIE irrespective of whether they audit the company (Grant Thornton says such a move “would be enormously detrimental to audit quality”)
- Splitting UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices (Grant Thornton warns this “would be enormously detrimental to audit quality” and risks mid-tier firms leaving the market)

Full answer
-----------

Deloitte rates High cost / Low benefit for these CMA measures:

- Break-up of the Big Four into smaller firms
- Insurance-based system
- Joint audits
- “NAO”-style national auditor
- Prohibit non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs
- Reform mandatory tendering/rotation
- Split UK firms into audit-only/non-audit practices

Of those, Grant Thornton says:

Could leave the market less concentrated

- Joint audits – Grant Thornton says they “generally result in a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services” (i.e. less concentrated).

Could reduce or destabilise audit quality

- “NAO”-style national auditor – Grant Thornton warns it is “not clear … how this measure would improve the quality of audits” and that it would lack “access to a major international network and access to the requisite specialists”.
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices – Grant Thornton warns that with firms “entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded”, and that splitting out audit is “unlikely to solve all the concerns” while risking the exit of mid-tier firms.

(Grant Thornton does not mention the other high-cost/low-benefit measures—break-up into smaller firms, insurance-based system, prohibiting NAS to large clients/PIEs, or reforming tendering/rotation—in terms of reducing concentration or destabilising quality.)

Document 4 is Grant Thornton UK LLP response. Key parts:

Executive summary:
- Agree competition not working well, remedies not impactful.
- Significant investments to grow presence in FTSE 350 audit market, understand barriers.
- Serious failings remain.
- Grant Thornton is fifth largest, particular viewpoint.
- They withdrew earlier 2018 from future tendering for FTSE 350 audit services because buyers' predisposition to Big 4 meant not level playing field.
- These problems specific to UK large company audit market, must be focus.
- Grant Thornton shares view unlikely one complete solution, range measures required.
- Caution with unintended consequences. Mid-sized and smaller companies operate in different market context. Should not disturb well-functioning market or act as barriers to expansion.
- Reform must focus on strengthening independence and removing biases in favour of Big 4 not driven by quality.
- Grant Thornton has technical capability, scale and capacity to undertake high quality audits for many FTSE 350 today including international operations. Recognise largest and most complex companies may require auditor that can match size/complexity, or two or more under shared mandate.
- Grant Thornton does not consider separating audit and non-audit practices of Big 4 effective remedy. Audit quality enhanced by broader resources available in multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit unlikely to solve concerns; rather create group of Big 4 audit-only firms with same market concentration and same buy-side biases. Some mid-tier firms may leave market rather than split to compete for PIE audit mandates. Although splitting addresses conflicts/independence partially, support simpler solutions.
- While Grant Thornton benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory, see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients. Regulatory changes tightened controls but auditors continue to provide non-audit services often around 40% of audit fee (up to 70%). Perception that sales create conflicts: clear ban may serve public interest. But need careful definition to allow compatible services.
- Grant Thornton does not consider introduction of market share cap in relation to percentage of FTSE 350 audits carried out by Big 4 is, by itself, useful tool. Not clear how made to operate without allowing Big 4 to cherry pick most attractive and profitable audits. Grant Thornton does consider there may be some benefit in setting an aspirational market share target for an independent auditor appointment body (see below) to aim for.
- Grant Thornton considers advantages to joint or shared audits. Mechanism used in France, better market stability, fewer high-profile failures, greater public trust. Offers ability to draw wider technical knowledge and experience, promotes better quality outcomes, ensures higher level of rigour and independence, generally results in more dynamic market in terms of number of firms able to provide effective audit services. Joint audits could be set up in number of ways – support 'shared' audit model. Acknowledge joint audit criticised and resisted in past by buy-side, look forward discussing. In particular, introduction and use of joint audit is something that could be supported by independent auditor appointment body.
- Grant Thornton previously called for independent auditor appointment body. Note use embraced in UK public sector. Equivalent mechanism for appointment of auditors to certain FTSE 250 companies could have positive effect in promoting independence and addressing potential bias. Although significant change, would send clear signal UK serious about market reform and ensuring firm appointed is best placed balancing interests of stakeholders and placing public interest at heart. Could also deal with non-audit service procurement independently addressing concerns over conflicts. Do not support nationalised provision.
- Effective reforms increasing competition and auditor independence would be strongly supported and encourage more non-Big 4 firms to tender for FTSE 350. Successful outcome: audit quality overall in FTSE 350 improves consistently; 10-20% of FTSE 350 audited by non-Big 4 within 3-4 years; wider stakeholder engagement; auditor independence strengthened; public trust restored.

Full answer
-----------

The Deloitte appendix rates High cost / Low benefit for these CMA measures:

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
- Joint audits
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
- Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices
- Insurance-based system
- “NAO” style national auditor

Of those, Grant Thornton says the following could leave the audit market less concentrated (alphabetically):

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (listed benefit: “Immediate impact on market concentration”)
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (listed benefit: Big 4 leaving for advisory work could lead to “less audit market concentration and more competition”)

And Grant Thornton warns the following could reduce or destabilise audit quality (alphabetically):

- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices (it argues audit quality is enhanced by multi-disciplinary resources and that splitting out audit removes those resources; it also notes some mid-tier firms may leave the market)

(The other High-cost/Low-benefit measures—joint audits, reform of tendering/rotation, insurance-based system and NAO-style auditor—are either not said by Grant Thornton to reduce concentration, or are not discussed by it in those terms; and Grant Thornton actually supports joint/shared audits rather than warning they harm quality.)

---------

Full answer
-----------

Deloitte rates “High” cost / “Low” benefit for these CMA measures:

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
- Insurance-based system
- Joint audits
- NAO-style national auditor
- Prohibit audit firms providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
- Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices

What Grant Thornton says about them

Could leave the audit market less concentrated:

- Joint audits – Grant Thornton says joint/shared audits “generally result in a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services” (and cites France, where the top four hold only ~47%).

Warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

- NAO-style national auditor – Grant Thornton states “private audit firms will provide the best quality audit in a well-functioning market” and does not support “the nationalised provision of audit services.”
- Split UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices – Grant Thornton argues that “audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights that are available… in a multi-disciplinary firm” and that splitting them out is “unlikely to solve all the concerns,” risks further eroding independence/incentives, and would be “enormously detrimental to audit quality.”

(Grant Thornton does not claim the other measures in this Deloitte “High-cost/Low-benefit” set—break-up of the Big Four, insurance-based system, prohibiting all non-audit services to large clients/PIEs, or reforming tender/rotation—would reduce concentration, nor does it warn they would destabilise quality in the visible response, though it rejects audit-only separation and nationalisation on quality grounds as noted above.)

In Executive Summary bullet points:
- Grant Thornton agrees competition not working well, remedies not impactful.
- They withdrew from future tendering for FTSE 350 audits.
- They think serious failings remain.
- Reform must focus on strengthening independence and removing biases in favour of Big 4.
- Grant Thornton does not consider separating audit and non-audit practices of Big 4 effective remedy. Audit quality enhanced by broader resources in multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit unlikely to solve concerns; rather create group of Big 4 audit-only firms with same market concentration and same buy-side biases. Some mid-tier firms may leave market, rather than split.
- While Grant Thornton benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory, see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients.
- Grant Thornton does not consider introduction of market share cap in relation to % of FTSE 350 audits carried out by Big 4 useful tool by itself. Not clear how made to operate without allowing Big 4 to cherry pick most attractive and profitable audits. Grant Thornton does consider there may be benefit in setting aspirational market share target for independent auditor appointment body.
- Grant Thornton does consider advantages to joint or shared audits. This mechanism used in countries like France, where better market stability, fewer high-profile failures, greater public trust. Offers ability to draw wider range of technical knowledge and experience, promotes better quality outcomes, ensures higher level of rigour and independence, generally results in more dynamic market in terms of number of firms able to provide effective audit services. Joint audits could be set up in number of ways – support 'shared' audit model. Acknowledge joint audit criticised and resisted in past by buy-side, but look forward to discussing.
- Grant Thornton previously called for independent auditor appointment body. Note use embraced in UK public sector. Equivalent mechanism for appointment of auditors to certain FTSE 250 companies could have positive effect in terms of promoting independence and addressing potential bias. Although significant change, would send clear signal UK serious about market reform and ensuring firm appointed is best placed to deliver audit, balancing interests and placing public interest at heart. Such arrangement could also deal with non-audit service procurement in fully independent manner addressing current concerns over conflicts (prohibition on selling non-audit services to PIE audit clients may also address this). Do not support nationalised provision of audit services.
- Effective reforms increasing competition and auditor independence in relation to PIEs strongly supported and would encourage more non-Big 4 firms to tender for FTSE 350 audits. Consider successful outcome would be: audit quality overall improves; 10-20% FTSE 350 audited by non-Big 4 within 3-4 years; wider stakeholder engagement; auditor independence strengthened; public trust restored.

This mechanism is used in countries such as France, where there is better market stability, with fewer high-profile failures and greater public trust in auditing. It offers the ability to draw on a wider range of technical knowledge and experience, promotes better quality outcomes, ensures a higher level of rigour and independence and generally results in a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services."

This suggests joint audits could lead to "a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services" which implies less concentration.
