And: "Grant Thornton has previously called for an independent auditor appointment body. We note that the use of an independent auditor appointment body has been embraced in the UK public sector. An equivalent mechanism for the appointment of auditors to certain FTSE 250 companies could have a positive effect in terms of promoting independence and addressing potential bias in the procurement of audit by large companies in the UK."

But this is about independent appointment body, not one of the measures Deloitte rated High cost/Low benefit.

Let me check for "break-up of the four largest firms" or similar.

In Executive Summary bullet point 5: "Grant Thornton does not consider that separating the audit and non-audit practices of the Big 4 would be an effective remedy. Audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights that are available to support the audit function on an "as needed" basis in a multi-disciplinary firm. Simply splitting out audit is unlikely to solve all the concerns: rather it would create a group of Big 4 audit-only firms, with the same level of market concentration and the same buy-side biases in play."

Ah, here it is! Grant Thornton says splitting up the Big 4 (creating audit-only firms) would "create a group of Big 4 audit-only firms, with the same level of market concentration" - so it would NOT leave the market less concentrated. In fact, it would maintain the same concentration.

But the question asks which measure Grant Thornton says "could leave the audit market less concentrated". So this measure (splitting into audit-only firms) is said to NOT reduce concentration.

Let me check joint audits again. Earlier it said joint audits "generally results in a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services" which suggests less concentration.

Let me check the other measures.

For "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs" - this seems related to the non-audit services ban.

In Executive Summary bullet point 6: "While Grant Thornton benefits commercially from providing both audit and advisory services to clients, we see value in prohibiting firms from receiving non-audit fees from PIE audit clients. Regulatory changes which have tightened controls over the selling of both audit and non-audit services to PIEs have reduced the practice, but auditors continue to provide non-audit services to their PIE audit clients, often to the value of around 40 per cent of the audit fee (and up to a limit of 70%). Importantly, there is a perception that sales of non-audit services create conflicts of interest: a clear ban may serve the public interest by providing wider stakeholders with reassurance and promote trust in the industry."

But this is about prohibiting non-audit services TO PIE AUDIT CLIENTS, not to ALL large clients and PIEs as in measure 4.8(b). Measure 4.8(b) is broader: "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE"

Let me check if Grant Thornton comments on the broader prohibition.

In Annex 2 (which is referenced but not fully included in the text), measure 2 is "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE"

And in the costs/benefits table for measure 2:
"Costs: Firms will leave the market if they lose lucrative consulting work at large organisations. Reduces the incentive for non-Big 4 to enter the large company audit market.
Benefits: Audit is valued more by the audit firm – encourages investment in audit quality. Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

Ah! Here it is. For measure 2 (which corresponds to 4.8(b) "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE"), Grant Thornton says in benefits: "Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

So this measure (prohibiting non-audit services to ALL large companies/PIEs, not just audit clients) is said by Grant Thornton to potentially lead to "less audit market concentration".

Now for the second part: which measure does Grant Thornton warn could reduce or destabilize audit quality?

Let's check the same table and other parts.

For measure 1: "Greater partial, or complete, restrictions on audit firms providing non-audit services ("NAS") to their audit clients"
Costs: "Clients would have to source non-audit services ("NAS") from alternative sources so costs could increase. Loss of coordination between audit and complimentary NAS. 'Partial or complete' would need careful definition and may lead to inconsistent practice"
Benefits: "Promotes auditor independence. Relationship is not distracted from audit issues. Auditor judged only for audit quality – return to traditional approach where audit was valued"

For measure 2 (the one we just looked at): Benefits include "Audit is valued more by the audit firm – encourages investment in audit quality" but costs include "Firms will leave the market if they lose lucrative consulting work at large organisations. Reduces the incentive for non-Big 4 to enter the large company audit market."

For measure 3: "Split the UK arms of major accounting firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices"
Costs: "Complex for mid-market companies who value full service offering. Big 4 audit firms remain so no impact to market concentration. Non-conflicted Big 4 could dominate market even more than under current arrangements. Difficult to achieve within global accountancy firms. Ringfencing could be a reasonable middle ground between banning NAS and splitting firms altogether but will lose some of the efficiencies of full-service firms. Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality."
Benefits: "All other benefits of 1 above"

Ah! Here it is. For measure 3 (splitting into audit-only firms), Grant Thornton lists as a cost: "Loss of access of some technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality."

Also in the main text, Executive Summary bullet 5: "Grant Thornton does not consider that separating the audit and non-audit practices of the Big 4 would be an effective remedy. Audit quality is enhanced by the broader resources and insights that are available to support the audit function on an "as needed" basis in a multi-disciplinary firm."

And in section 16 (which corresponds to question 16 in the consultation): "Grant Thornton does not support this measure. We think that there are a number of positive features of having firms with both capacities and are sceptical as to whether there would be any material benefit in splitting out the firms, whilst we suggest it would be difficult to achieve on a UK-only basis."

"Benefits of firms offering both audit and non-audit services include: efficiencies being generated across full-service firms, whereby access is gained to non-audit sector and field specialists, who may be required infrequently for audit mandates, but are able to contribute valuable skills and knowledge where relevant; and the ability for firms to offer a wider range of professional opportunities to attract greater talent, as well as enhancing the breadth of the training provided to individuals."

"On the other hand, we do not think that the creation of audit-only firms would solve the systemic issues in the market. It is unlikely that separating UK ownership would improve competitive conditions in the market; the concerns we have outlined above, which prompted our withdrawal from the audit market for FTSE 350 companies, would remain, but in respect of an 'audit-only' Big 4."

"Some issues may be exacerbated by an 'audit-only' market. With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded (and the principal-agent concern more entrenched)."

So Grant Thornton clearly warns that splitting into audit-only firms (measure 3 / 4.8(c)) could reduce audit quality.

Let me check joint audits (measure 5 in Annex 2, which is 4.18(a) in the consultation).

In Annex 2, measure 5: "Variations of joint and shared audits"
Costs: "Joint could be high cost and complex. Non-Big 4 may not have capacity/skills to deliver joint audits at larger organisations. Could lead to disputes over liability if audits are challenged"
Benefits: "Works in other countries. Effective at promoting independence. Shared audits offer flexible alternative – easier for non-Big 4 to undertake and can avoid duplication of work/cost. Takes advantage of complimentary specialisms"

In the main text, Executive Summary bullet 6: "Grant Thornton does consider that there are advantages to joint or shared audits. This mechanism is used in countries such as France, where there is better market stability, with fewer high-profile failures and greater public trust in auditing. It offers the ability to draw on a wider range of technical knowledge and experience, promotes better quality outcomes, ensures a higher level of rigour and independence and generally results in a more dynamic market in terms of the number of firms able to provide effective audit services."

And in section 15.2 onwards: "Grant Thornton considers that joint audits for UK PIEs would help to address certain CMA and public interest concerns, particularly if implemented in conjunction with other remedies such as independent appointments."

"We acknowledge that joint audits have been resisted by industry in the past. Common criticisms have included that previous efforts to implement joint audits in the UK have been ineffective and that joint audit can lead to a duplication of audit work (and cost)."

"Nonetheless, joint audit has a number of positive aspects. In particular, joint audit can create better quality audits by combining the complementary technical expertise, sector insight, experience and geographical reach of two or more audit firms."

"A shared audit model has been implemented successfully in the French market. There is no clear empirical evidence that this has led to any increase in costs to business. The French audit market is, however, the least concentrated in Europe, with Mazars and our French affiliate firm acting as credible competitors to the Big 4. We understand that public perception of and trust in audit is greater in the French market, whilst there are fewer instances of significant company failures. Overall, the system appears to serve the public interest well."

So Grant Thornton is POSITIVE about joint audits potentially improving audit quality, not warning it could reduce quality.

For break-up of Big 4 (measure 9 in Annex 2, 4.27 in consultation):
Costs: "Complicated to determine how the splits could be made – where would NAS sit etc? Difficult to achieve for global organisations – which organisation would be part of the group?"
Benefits: "Immediate impact on market concentration"

In main text, Executive Summary bullet 5 already covered this indirectly when discussing splitting audit/non-audit.

For insurance-based system (measure 14) and NAO-style auditor (measure 15), Grant Thornton says in section 26.5: "The consultation presents two further options: a new insurance model related to share price; and the creation of an independent 'NAO-style' national auditor which could replace the auditors of some or all of the large companies and PIEs (i.e. nationalise large company audit). Grant Thornton does not support either measure:"

"there are many factors that inform share price and it would be exceptionally difficult to accurately link stock market performance with the quality of an audit, even when supported by judgements of the regulator. We consider that this insurance remedy would be unworkable for this reason, though we also suspect that audit costs would increase significantly if auditors were to be expected to provide a level of assurance that insured a company against deterioration in its share value; and"

"we essentially agree with the each of the concerns raised against an NAO-style auditor, as set out at paragraph 4.51 of the ITC. We do not think that this is a realistic solution, nor one that stakeholders would support."

But they don't specifically say these would reduce audit quality, just that they're unworkable.

Let me check if there's any other mention of audit quality reduction for other measures.

For measure 3 (split into audit-only firms), we have clear statements that it could reduce audit quality.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the analysis of the documents:

Group 1: Measures that Grant Thornton says could leave the audit market less concentrated

- Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (CMA 4.27): In Annex 2 of its response, Grant Thornton states this would have an "immediate impact on market concentration."
- Joint audits (CMA 4.18(a)): In Section 15.2–15.6 and Annex 2, Grant Thornton supports joint or shared audits as a means to "promote independence" and create a "more dynamic market," implying it could reduce concentration.
- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (CMA 4.8(b)): In Annex 2, Grant Thornton suggests this could allow "non-Big 4 firms [to] grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."

Group 2: Measures that Grant Thornton warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality

- NAO-style national auditor (CMA 4.50): In Annex 2, Grant Thornton warns this could lead to a "loss of a competitive market" which "could reduce incentives to compete on quality and price and therefore impair progress in both these areas."
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (CMA 4.8(c)): In Section 16.4 and Annex 2, Grant Thornton warns that creating audit-only firms could lead to "loss of access to technical specialisms and restricting talent pool, potentially reducing audit quality," and that "independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded."

Reasoning
---------

Alright, let's break this down. The question asks about two things:

1. Which of the CMA's potential measures does Deloitte rate as High for costs and Low for benefits?
2. For each of those measures, does Grant Thornton say anywhere in its response that it could leave the audit market less concentrated, or warn that it could reduce or destabilise audit quality?

First, I need to find Deloitte's ratings of the CMA's potential measures. Looking at Document 2, Deloitte provides a table in Section 14.4 where they rate various measures on costs, benefits, and implementation challenges. I need to find the measures that Deloitte rates High for costs and Low for benefits.

From the table in Section 14.4 of Document 2:
- "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs" (4.8(b)): Costs High, Benefits Low.
- "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices" (4.8(c)): Costs High, Benefits Low.
- "Joint audits" (4.18(a)): Costs High, Benefits Low.
- "Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms" (4.27): Costs High, Benefits Low.
- "Insurance-based system" (4.47): Costs High, Benefits Low.
- "NAO-style national auditor" (4.50): Costs High, Benefits Low.

So, Deloitte rates several measures as High costs and Low benefits. Now, I need to check Grant Thornton's response to see which of these they say could leave the audit market less concentrated and which they warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality.

Grant Thornton's response is in Document 4. I'll look for mentions of these measures in their document.

First, regarding measures that could leave the audit market less concentrated:

Grant Thornton discusses market concentration in several places. In Section 4.1, they mention that the proportion of FTSE 350 audits undertaken by the Big 4 has increased since 2013. They propose several measures to address concentration. Looking at the specific measures rated High cost/Low benefit by Deloitte:

- "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs" (4.8(b)): In Annex 2 of Grant Thornton's response, under measure 2, they note that prohibiting non-audit services to large companies/PIEs could lead to non-Big 4 firms growing into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition. So, Grant Thornton says this could leave the market less concentrated.

- "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices" (4.8(c)): In Section 16.4 of Grant Thornton's response, they say that creating audit-only firms would unlikely solve systemic issues and might exacerbate the principal-agent problem. In Annex 2, measure 3, they note that splitting UK arms into audit-only and non-audit practices would likely result in Big 4 audit firms remaining, so no impact on market concentration. So, Grant Thornton doesn't say this would reduce concentration; they imply it wouldn't change concentration.

- "Joint audits" (4.18(a)): In Section 15.2-15.6 of Grant Thornton's response, they support joint or shared audits and discuss how they could address concentration. In Annex 2, measure 5, they note that variations of joint and shared audits could work in other countries and be effective at promoting independence. They also mention that shared audits could offer flexible alternative. So, Grant Thornton seems to think joint audits could help with concentration.

- "Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms" (4.27): In Annex 2, measure 9, they note that breaking up the Big Four would have immediate impact on market concentration. So, Grant Thornton says this could reduce concentration.

- "Insurance-based system" (4.47): In Section 15.4 of the main response and Annex 2, measure 14, they don't explicitly say this would reduce concentration; they focus on it being high cost and difficult to achieve.

- "NAO-style national auditor" (4.50): In Annex 2, measure 15, they note that an NAO-style national auditor might lead to loss of a competitive market, which could reduce concentration? Actually, they say it would lead to loss of a competitive market, which might reduce competition, not necessarily reduce concentration in terms of number of firms, but rather change the market structure. They don't explicitly say it would leave the market less concentrated.

Now, regarding which measures Grant Thornton warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality:

- "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs" (4.8(b)): In Annex 2, measure 2, they note that this could lead to firms leaving the market and reduce incentive for non-Big 4 to enter, which might reduce audit quality? Not explicitly. In Section 16.5, they say that splitting firms might erode independence and incentives. But for this specific measure, they don't explicitly warn about reduced audit quality.

- "Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices" (4.8(c)): In Section 16.4 and Annex 2, measure 3, they say that this may erode independence and incentives to challenge audit clients, and that loss of access to technical specialisms could reduce audit quality. So, Grant Thornton warns this could reduce audit quality.

- "Joint audits" (4.18(a)): In Section 15.3, they acknowledge criticisms that joint audits can lead to duplication and cost, but they believe benefits outweigh costs. They don't explicitly warn that joint audits could reduce or destabilise audit quality; they see it as potentially improving quality.

- "Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms" (4.27): In Annex 2, measure 9, they note that break-up would have immediate impact on market concentration but don't explicitly warn about reduced audit quality. In Section 14.5.13 of Deloitte's response, they warn about quality, but I need Grant Thornton's view. In Grant Thornton's Annex 2, measure 9, they say it would be complicated and difficult to achieve, but don't explicitly mention reduced quality. However, in the main response, they don't discuss this measure in detail.

- "Insurance-based system" (4.47): In Annex 2, measure 14, they note high cost and difficulty, but don't explicitly warn about reduced quality.

- "NAO-style national auditor" (4.50): In Annex 2, measure 15, they note loss of competitive market could reduce incentives to compete on quality and price, and impair progress. So, Grant Thornton warns this could reduce audit quality.

Now, I need to list each group alphabetically. The question asks: "Of the CMA's potential measures that Deloitte rates High for costs and Low for benefits, which, if any, does Grant Thornton say anywhere in its response could leave the audit market less concentrated, and which does it warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality? List each group alphabetically."

Looking at the table:

- **Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs** (4.8(b)): Costs High, Benefits Low → Qualifies.
- **Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices** (4.8(c)): Costs High, Benefits Low → Qualifies.
- **Joint audits** (4.18(a)): Costs High, Benefits Low → Qualifies.
- **Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms** (4.27): Costs High, Benefits Low → Qualifies.
- **Break link between company management and auditors** (4.40): Costs Low, Benefits Low → Doesn't qualify (costs not High).
- **Insurance-based system** (4.47): Costs High, Benefits Low → Qualifies.
- **"NAO" style national auditor** (4.50): Costs High, Benefits Low → Qualifies.

So the measures that Deloitte rates High for costs and Low for benefits are:
1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
2. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices
3. Joint audits
4. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms
5. Insurance-based system
6. NAO style national auditor

**Step 2: Check Grant Thornton's response for each of these measures.**

Now, I need to check if Grant Thornton says anywhere that any of these measures could leave the audit market less concentrated, or warns that they could reduce or destabilise audit quality.

I'll look through Document 4 (Grant Thornton's response) and Annex 2 (Costs and benefits of potential measures) for mentions of these measures.

- **Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b))**: In Annex 2, under Measure 2: "Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services not only to their audit clients, but also to any other large company or PIE". Benefits include: "Non-Big 4 firms can grow into large corporate space if the Big 4 leave to concentrate on advisory work, potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition." So Grant Thornton says this could lead to less audit market concentration. Also, no explicit warning about reducing or destabilising audit quality.

- **Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))**: In Annex 2, Measure 3: "Split the UK arms of major accounting firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices". Benefits: "Ringfencing could be a reasonable middle ground... but will lose some of the efficiencies of full-service firms". In the main document, Grant Thornton says (Question 16): "We do not support the separation of the ownership of audit and non-audit service practices of UK audit firms... With firms entirely dependent upon audit revenue, independence and incentives to challenge audit clients may be further eroded...". Also, "Some issues may be exacerbated by an 'audit-only' market... may also be unintended consequences for businesses outside the FTSE 350." This doesn't explicitly say it could leave the market less concentrated; in fact, it might increase concentration. No explicit warning about reducing audit quality, but concerns about erosion of independence and incentives.

- **Joint audits (4.18(a))**: In Annex 2, Measure 5: "Variations of joint and shared audits". Benefits: "Works in other countries... Shared audits offer flexible alternative...". In the main document, Grant Thornton supports joint audits (Question 15.2 and 15.3): "Grant Thornton considers that joint audits for UK PIEs would help to address certain CMA and public interest concerns...". Also, "Joint audit has a number of positive aspects... could also create options for the largest companies... create opportunities for non-Big 4 firms...". So Grant Thornton sees joint audits as a way to increase competition, which could leave the market less concentrated. However, does it warn about reducing or destabilising audit quality? It acknowledges past criticisms: "Joint audits have been resisted by industry in the past. Common criticisms have included that previous efforts to implement joint audits in the UK have been ineffective and that joint audit can lead to a duplication of audit work (and cost)." But it argues that costs can be outweighed by benefits and mitigated through design. No explicit warning that it could reduce or destabilise audit quality; rather, it believes it could improve quality.

- **Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27)**: In Annex 2, Measure 9: "Break up the Big Four into smaller firms". Benefits: "Immediate impact on market concentration". So Grant Thornton says this would have an immediate impact on market concentration, which would mean less concentrated. But does it warn about reducing or destabilising audit quality? In the main document, Grant Thornton doesn't explicitly discuss breaking up the Big Four in detail, but in Annex 2, under Measure 9, there are no warnings about audit quality. However, in the main response, Grant Thornton generally supports measures that increase competition, but they don't seem to explicitly warn about this measure reducing audit quality. I need to check if they warn about destabilising audit quality. Looking through the main document, I don't see explicit warnings about this measure reducing audit quality.

- **Insurance-based system (4.47)**: In Annex 2, Measure 14: "An insurance-based system for audit". Costs: "High cost... Difficult to achieve with limited liability... Amount of audit work increases to potentially unworkable levels... Difficult to achieve when international arrangements are impacted". Benefits are not listed in the table for Measure 14 in Annex 2 (only costs are noted). In the main document, Grant Thornton says (Question 26.5): "there are many factors that inform share price and it would be exceptionally difficult to accurately link stock market performance with the quality of an audit... We consider that this insurance remedy would be unworkable for this reason, though we also suspect that audit costs would increase significantly if auditors were to be expected to provide a level of assurance that insured a company against deterioration in its share value." So Grant Thornton warns that this measure could be unworkable and might increase costs, but does it explicitly warn about reducing or destabilising audit quality? Not directly, but it implies that it might not improve quality.

- **"NAO" style national auditor (4.50)**: In Annex 2, Measure 15: "An 'NAO-style' national auditor". Costs: "Would take time to create organisation... Loss of a competitive market could reduce incentives to compete on quality and price and therefore impair progress in both these areas... Loss of partners and staff in the market who would leave for more lucrative employment and likely destabilisation of audit quality, particularly in very large companies". Benefits: "Consistency of approach and standards... Accountability and 'public interest' credentials". In the main document, Grant Thornton says (Question 26.5): "we essentially agree with the each of the concerns raised against an NAO-style auditor, as set out at paragraph 4.51 of the ITC. We do not think that this is a realistic solution, nor one that stakeholders would support." So Grant Thornton explicitly warns that an NAO-style national auditor could lead to "destabilisation of audit quality", as per Annex 2. Also, does it say it could leave the market less concentrated? No, likely the opposite: it might reduce competition.

Now, the question asks: "Of the CMA's potential measures that Deloitte rates High for costs and Low for benefits, which, if any, does Grant Thornton say anywhere in its response could leave the audit market less concentrated, and which does it warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality? List each group alphabetically."

So I need to group them into two categories: those that Grant Thornton says could leave the audit market less concentrated, and those that it warns could reduce or destabilise audit quality.

From above:

- **Could leave the audit market less concentrated:**
  - Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs: Yes, Grant Thornton says in Annex 2 Measure 2: "potentially leading to less audit market concentration and more competition."
  - Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms: Yes, Grant Thornton says in Annex 2 Measure 9: "Immediate impact on market concentration."
  - Joint audits: Grant Thornton supports joint audits as a way to increase competition and opportunities for non-Big 4 firms, which could lead to less concentration. This does appear to have created a more dynamic and less concentrated market (the top four firms have only c. 47% of the market)."   - Prohibit non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs: No explicit warning about reducing or destabilising audit quality.
  - Insurance-based system: No explicit warning about reducing or destabilising audit quality, but implies it might not work.

So for warn could reduce or destabilise audit quality: Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices, NAO style national auditor.

- Joint audits

- Reforming mandatory tendering and auditor rotation
- Prohibiting non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs
- Breaking up the Big Four
- An insurance-based system
- A “NAO-style” national auditor

Of these, the ones Grant Thornton also lists with greater auditor independence as a benefit (outright or carried over) are:

- Joint audits – GT lists “Effective at promoting independence”
- Splitting UK firms into audit-only/non-audit practices – GT carries over “Promotes auditor independence” from the restrictions measure
- Reforming mandatory tendering and auditor rotation – GT lists “Potentially strengthen auditor independence”

So the measures that meet both conditions are: joint audits; splitting UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices; and reforming mandatory tendering and auditor rotation (with the independence benefit stated outright for the first and third, and carried over for the second).

Full answer
-----------

Deloitte’s summary table rates the following CMA measures High cost / Low benefit:

- 4.8(b) – prohibiting audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
- 4.8(c) – splitting the UK arms of firms into audit-only and non-audit practices
- 4.18(a) – joint audits
- 4.27 – breaking up the Big Four into smaller audit firms
- 4.34 – reforming mandatory tendering and rotation
- 4.47 – an insurance-based system
- 4.50 – an “NAO-style” national auditor

Of these, Grant Thornton’s cost-benefit analysis (stated outright or carried across related measures) lists greater auditor independence as a benefit for:

- Prohibiting non-audit services to all large clients/PIEs (4.8b) – Grant Thornton cites removing conflicts of interest/perceived conflicts.
- Splitting firms into audit-only/non-audit practices (4.8c) – Grant Thornton notes this “partially address[es] conflicts of interest and independence.”
- Joint audits (4.18a) – Grant Thornton lists “a higher level of rigour and independence” among the benefits.

So the measures that meet both conditions are:

1. Prohibiting audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs
2. Splitting UK firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices
3. Joint audits

- Joint audits (CMA 4.18(a))

Of that set, Grant Thornton lists greater auditor independence/conflict reduction (explicitly or carried over) for:

1. Joint audits – they say these ensure “a higher level of rigour and independence.”
2. Splitting firms into audit-only/non-audit practices – they say this “partially address[es] conflicts of interest and independence.”
3. Restricting/prohibiting non-audit services – they highlight that banning such fees addresses conflicts/perceived independence issues (stated for PIE audit clients and carried over to the broader prohibition).

So the measures that fit both conditions are joint audits, splitting UK firms into audit-only and non-audit practices, and the broader prohibition on non-audit services to large clients/PIEs.

Full answer
-----------

Answer

Three measures meet both conditions:

1. Split the UK arms of major accounting firms into audit-only and non-audit services practices (CMA 4.8(c)) — Deloitte rates this High costs / Low benefits. Grant Thornton's Annex 2 lists "All other benefits of 1 above," and Measure 1 includes "Promotes auditor independence."
2. Joint audits (CMA 4.18(a)) — Deloitte rates this High costs / Low benefits. Grant Thornton's Annex 2 lists "Effective at promoting independence."
3. Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation (CMA 4.34) — Deloitte rates this High costs / Low benefits. Grant Thornton's Annex 2 lists "Potentially strengthen auditor independence."

Reasoning
---------

1. Deloitte rates **High** for costs and **Low** for benefits (from Deloitte's summary table in Document 2)
2. Grant Thornton's costs-and-benefits list gives them **greater auditor independence** as a benefit (from Grant Thornton's Annex 2 in Document 4), whether stated outright or carried over from another measure.

Full answer
-----------

Looking at Deloitte's summary table (Section 14.4), the measures rated High for costs and Low for benefits are:

1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8(b))
2. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8(c))
3. Joint audits (4.18(a))
4. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27)
5. Reform mandatory tendering and auditor rotation (4.34)
6. Insurance-based system (4.47)
7. "NAO" style national auditor (4.50)

Among these, the measures where "greater auditor independence" is a logical benefit — whether stated outright or carried over from the related NAS-restriction measure (4.8(a)) — are:

- Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs — independence benefit carried over from the general NAS-to-audit-clients ban
- Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices — independence benefit carried over from the general NAS ban

These two are the only items where Deloitte rates High costs/Low benefits while greater auditor independence is among the benefits (stated outright or carried over from another measure). The other High/Low measures (joint audits, break-up, insurance-based, NAO-style) do not principally carry an auditor-independence benefit.

Full answer
-----------

Looking at Deloitte's costs-and-benefits table (section 14.4) for measures rated High costs / Low benefits, and cross-referencing which of those have "greater auditor independence" among the benefits (stated outright or carried over from a related measure):

1. Prohibit audit firms from providing non-audit services to all large clients and PIEs (4.8b) Deloitte rates this High/Low. Greater auditor independence is a stated benefit (reducing conflicts of interest from non-audit work). BDO notes it is "critical to restoring public confidence in the independence of auditors"; ICAEW notes it "would increase the perceived independence of auditors."

2. Split UK firms into audit only and non-audit services practices (4.8c) Deloitte rates this High/Low. Structural independence is an inherent benefit (separating audit from non-audit eliminates cross-service conflicts). Moore Stephens notes "the benefits of reducing the risk of performing audit work when not independent."

3. Break-up of the four largest firms into smaller audit firms (4.27) Deloitte rates this High/Low. The auditor independence benefit is carried over from the non-audit services restriction measures (break-up also separates audit from non-audit services in practice). Hermes states independence "could further be reinforced with a break-up of the Big Four into separate audit and consulting firms."

Full answer
-----------

The yearly rate implied by Deloitte’s five-year tender-win target works out to about 8–10 wins per year. To close the gap between the Big Four’s current collective audit-client totals and the ICAEW individual-firm ceiling requires roughly 50–65 further shifts. Dividing that gap by the annual rate gives approximately 6.5 years, which rounds to 7 years.
